## AI Research

### [Loss of Oversight: How AI Systems May Become Harder to Audit, Monitor, and Investigate](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JvZxp554WxcZ8BQvM/loss-of-oversight-how-ai-systems-may-become-harder-to-audit-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Produced by </span></i><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>UK AISI</span></i></a><i><span> Model Transparency and Situational Awareness teams. If you’re a Research Scientist or Research Engineer, we’re hiring – apply </span></i><a href="https://job-boards.eu.greenhouse.io/aisi/jobs/4848454101" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><i><span>here</span></i></a><i><span> and come and work with us! </span></i></p><h3><b><span>TL;DR </span></b></h3><ul><li value="1"><span>We wrote a </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><span>report</span></a><span> on risks to AI oversight (auditing, monitoring, incident investigation), informed by interviewing many researchers (Figure 1 below), and our own analysis. </span></li><li value="2"><span>We find that many of the properties relied on for current oversight face a range of likely and potentially severe degradation pathways.</span><b><span> Much oversight rests on foundations that are likely to erode, absent effective intervention. </span></b></li><li value="3"><span>We give specific recommendations for measuring shifts in oversight-relevant properties, working to preserve oversight, and investing in emerging oversight techniques as fallbacks against continued degradation.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The full report can be accessed at </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></li></ul><img alt="Screenshot 2026-05-20 at 11.47.50.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779274152/lexical_client_uploads/r2o05zeseui3u5mqujg7.png" /><p><i><span>Figure 1: A list of the experts interviewed to inform the content of this </span></i><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>report</span></i></a><i><span>. ∗Some experts preferred not to be named, and have not been included in this list.</span></i></p><h3><br /><b><span>My informal LessWrong blurb</span></b></h3><p><i><span>This reflects only the personal view of Jordan Taylor, not the interviewed experts or UK AISI more broadly.</span></i></p><p><span>Right now, it seems we have pretty decent oversight. Not great, not terrible:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>When AIs deliberately do bad things, they can usually be caught.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo4vgs0kgb2d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno4vgs0kgb2d">[1]</a></sup></span><span> e.g. by CoT monitoring, or by them admitting to it.</span></li><li value="2"><span>When AIs are designed with hidden goals or propensities that they are introspectively aware of, they can usually be found e.g. by </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/index.html#nlas-are-competitive-on-a-downstream-auditing-task" rel="noreferrer"><span>white-box</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/introspection-adapters/" rel="noreferrer"><span>techniques</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10965" rel="noreferrer"><span>prefill attacks</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref18ggwqsczvs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn18ggwqsczvs">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>But there are a bunch of ways in which we might be playing on easy-mode today, relative to how difficult oversight like this could be in the future. The dominant problem is of course just generally increasing model capabilities for subversion, situational awareness, and doing tasks which are harder for humans to understand or oversee. But there are also a lot more acute (and potentially more preventable) risks to oversight. We compile these and give suggestions for tracking and preventing them. </span></p><h3><b><span>My recommendations for LessWrong readers</span></b></h3><p><span>The full </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><span>report</span></a><span> gives a lot of recommendations for model developers, but here I'll list some recommendations I'm most excited about for general AI safety researchers:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Monitorability benchmarking (</span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1dfIyuaUE65-qQy1U5fNNEYhKSOUhP55clnJjdcogqbs/edit?usp=sharing" rel="noreferrer"><span>we're currently working on this</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Building upon or red-teaming training-based approaches for monitorability, e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08093" rel="noreferrer"><span>Confession</span></a><span> / </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22303" rel="noreferrer"><span>Self-Incrimination</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=sWs0cCuM8I" rel="noreferrer"><span>training</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.13551" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tandem Training</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.13692" rel="noreferrer"><span>Prover-verifier games,</span></a><span> Honest </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01689" rel="noreferrer"><span>character training</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>NLAs</span></a></li><li value="3"><span>Understanding which kinds of training degrade monitorability </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqxaybamx5ar"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqxaybamx5ar">[3]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><span>Measuring </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15515" rel="noreferrer"><span>representational drift</span></a><span>, seeing if concepts change across RL training and if this makes it harder to train effective whitebox techniques afterwards</span></li><li value="5"><span>Creating model organisms of the degradation pathways below</span></li><li value="6"><span>Investigating which monitoring approaches are correlated with others, and likely to have shared failures, or to degrade simultaneously for the same reasons</span></li><li value="7"><span>Looking at neglected areas of oversight, e.g. incident response / logging / reproducibility, identifying diffuse patterns, monitoring the training process for bad incentives, etc.</span></li><li value="8"><span>Pursuing further </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ekp-eg7TcIY" rel="noreferrer"><span>governance-adjacent technical research</span></a><span> </span></li></ul><h1><span>Executive Summary</span></h1><p><b><span>The safety of advanced AI systems depends on the ability to oversee them.</span></b><span> As AI systems become more capable of causing harm, safety arguments increasingly draw on evidence from </span><i><span>oversight</span></i><span> — the ability to detect undesired behaviours or properties. This can include </span><i><span>auditing</span></i><span> models for misalignment before deployment, </span><i><span>monitoring</span></i><span> for problems during training or deployment, and </span><i><span>investigating incidents</span></i><span> after they occur.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpivk34b7kl9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpivk34b7kl9">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span>This </span></b><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><b><span>report</span></b></a><b><span> examines how oversight is likely to degrade, and what can be done to preserve or improve oversight as AI systems develop.</span></b><span> It draws on interviews with 25 experts across frontier AI developers, government, NGOs, and academia, as well as a literature review and our own analysis. Our goal is to inform our own research priorities and contribute to a shared understanding of what effective oversight requires. </span></p><p><b><span>Our key takeaway is that current oversight rests on contingent properties of AI systems, many of which are likely to erode.</span></b><span> For example, today's models often reason in human-readable text, allowing monitoring of complex reasoning. These properties underpin the evidence used in safety cases and risk assessments, but many are likely to degrade as training is scaled up, as models become more capable, as they are deployed with greater autonomy into increasingly complex environments, and as new approaches to building AI systems gain traction. Across oversight domains (illustrated in Figure 2), we identify more than twenty distinct degradation pathways. While any particular degradation pathway is uncertain, the aggregated evidence suggests that </span><b><span>current oversight is likely to degrade absent intervention.</span></b><span> New oversight methods which could compensate are not yet mature enough to take their place. Despite this, there are promising interventions to preserve existing oversight for longer and to hasten the development of emerging oversight techniques, which we discuss throughout the full </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><span>report</span></a><span>.</span></p><img alt="oversight-surfaces-v3.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779270788/lexical_client_uploads/wazc1c8qc4kmjjbn1tmj.png" /><p><i><span>Figure 2: Overview of oversight surfaces and techniques. Oversight surfaces (top) represent distinct parts of the AI system that yield oversight signal. Oversight techniques (bottom) are applied to these surfaces as layered defences, grouped by report section. Training-based approaches (bottom row) cut across multiple surfaces. The listed oversight techniques are illustrative and not exhaustive.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Summary of degradation pathways</span></h2><p><span>The key properties that current oversight relies on all face credible degradation pathways, some of which are already underway. The tables below show only the highest-severity and highest-likelihood pathways; additional pathways with lower severity or likelihood are discussed in the corresponding body sections.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdhibdx10zv5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndhibdx10zv5">[5]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref97yltylehzd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn97yltylehzd">[6]</a></sup></span></p><h3><b><span>Chain-of-thought reasoning is currently the most informative monitoring signal, but it is under significant pressure.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmgj2c26oefj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmgj2c26oefj">[7]</a></sup></span></h3><p><span>Chain-of-thought (CoT) is the step-by-step reasoning text that current models produce. CoT currently provides a useful monitoring surface across a range of risk models, including for detecting reward hacking, and evaluation gaming. It is among the most compelling sources of evidence for convincing decision-makers of the need to take action.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd9dryndjsh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd9dryndjsh">[8]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779278737/lexical_client_uploads/kpirrswr5d053wjwozkw.png" /><p><br /></p><h3><b><span>Action-only monitoring provides a floor for oversight, but it is not sufficient on its own.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref88ugkvla3zr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn88ugkvla3zr">[9]</a></sup></span></h3><p><span>Action-only monitoring is observing only what a model does, rather than its reasoning. It provides a baseline level of oversight for tasks where harmful actions are obviously incriminating.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779279216/lexical_client_uploads/w5bbdan9xxs58sdlxrdx.png" /><p><br /></p><h3><b><span>Evaluation gaming is a growing threat to auditing.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftq4i93af42d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntq4i93af42d">[10]</a></sup></span></h3><p><span>Evaluations are used to audit models for undesired capabilities, propensities, or misalignment. Evaluation gaming is when a model identifies when it is being evaluated and modifies its behaviour accordingly. It is already threatening to undermine the validity of some alignment audits.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref11txjzdgv7b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn11txjzdgv7b">[11]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbd8frq70vom"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbd8frq70vom">[12]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779356493/lexical_client_uploads/wrw15reugyr9x9bd5cbx.png" /><p><br /></p><h3><b><span>Changes in architecture for memory and learning could undermine oversight</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8n67ukbtzze"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8n67ukbtzze">[13]</a></sup></span></h3><p><span>Current AI systems store memories and communicate with other systems in text, supporting oversight. In future, AI systems might store memories in less interpretable formats such as vector memory and/or continue to update model weights between deployment sessions.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779278909/lexical_client_uploads/mkg2id66vs55cbggbckq.png" /><p><br /></p><h3><b><span>White-box methods are a promising backstop, but are not yet mature enough to compensate for degradation elsewhere</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzpyuccqip1d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzpyuccqip1d">[14]</a></sup></span></h3><p><span>White-box methods examine a model's internal representations, rather than what the model reveals in its outputs or reasoning. They span a wide maturity spectrum: simple techniques such as linear probes are starting to be deployed for some production use cases (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11516" rel="noreferrer"><span>Kramár et al., 2026</span></a><span>), while more advanced techniques are used experimentally for auditing but are not yet mature enough for broad safety-critical uses.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779278950/lexical_client_uploads/egtx7qrqtj12o9d9nsrj.png" /><h3><br /><b><span>Training-based approaches are promising but face fundamental challenges around generalisation</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2vfhyqmgw4q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2vfhyqmgw4q">[15]</a></sup></span></h3><p><span>Training-based approaches aim to produce models that are more honest and forthcoming, or serve as better monitors, creating new sources of oversight signal such as confessions. Most approaches to training for oversight are early-stage, but many experts felt that with sufficient effort they could be developed into a central pillar of oversight as necessity-based CoT monitorability degrades.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuxonylt4k9g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuxonylt4k9g">[16]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779279005/lexical_client_uploads/zqvdy50alkbnwwpi8nnk.png" /><p><br /></p><h2><span>Expert disagreements</span></h2><p><span>Along the way, we highlight many unresolved disagreements between experts. LessWrong is a natural place to discuss how these disagreements could be resolved, so leave a comment if you feel you can help! Some examples of disagreements are: </span></p><p><b><span>Whether latent reasoning architectures will take over.</span></b><span> </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779277845/lexical_client_uploads/qkia9ktgjbmucvjywhsy.png" /><p><b><span>On the extent and impact of non-human concepts:</span></b></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779278118/lexical_client_uploads/fgixcxfa7zqyvqqnoxcb.png" /><p><b><span>Whether evidence from misalignment honeypots is meaningful:</span></b></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779278575/lexical_client_uploads/j3zsefzbjlwi1z2xzyiv.png" /><p><b><span>Whether training for oversight will generalise to deployment contexts, given potential training-gaming</span></b><span>:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779278192/lexical_client_uploads/uovjxargewkmhymrq0uj.png" /><p><br /></p><h2><span>Our Recommendations</span></h2><p><span>In addition to the degradation pathways for current oversight, experts were also asked about potential solutions. Detailed technical levers for preserving or enhancing oversight are discussed throughout the </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><span>report</span></a><span>. In addition, the following ideas emerged as high-level recommendations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref27s4w1kzznj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn27s4w1kzznj">[17]</a></sup></span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Implement and maintain a diverse portfolio of currently available oversight techniques.</span></b><span> Some past oversight failures could likely have been prevented by implementing existing techniques. Developers should deploy a diverse set of monitoring and auditing techniques including CoT monitoring, action-only monitoring and AI control, evaluations, and white-box methods such as probes to provide defence in depth against gaps in any single oversight surface.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Track, report and forecast shifts in oversight-relevant properties.</span></b><span> Many oversight-relevant properties (e.g. single-forward-pass capabilities, representational drift, ability to bypass monitors) require explicit continued measurement. Model developers should be clear about which oversight properties they are relying on when making safety claims, and assess their exposure to degradations in these properties. Risk assessments should include forward projections of whether key properties will be preserved, so that safety arguments do not silently expire.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Preserve oversight by design</span></b><span> where possible. Developers should hold-out the most informative and fragile monitoring surfaces from training, preserving them as independent validation channels. The held-out sources should be independent from the sources that are trained against, in the sense that they aren't likely to fail for the same reasons. Beyond this, developers should preserve text-based reasoning and memory architectures where possible, and maintain logs sufficient to reproduce and investigate concerning model behaviours.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Research emerging oversight techniques</span></b><span> in case current oversight channels continue to degrade. Priority areas include: training for honesty and faithfulness; scaling-up pragmatic white-box methods and further developing more ambitious techniques; developing the science of AI control for action-only monitoring; and mitigating evaluation gaming with production-realistic evaluations and other tools.</span></li></ol><p><span>We provide more specific research recommendations in the </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/will-it-become-harder-to-oversee-ai-systems" rel="noreferrer"><span>full report</span></a><span> (section 7.3).</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fno4vgs0kgb2d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo4vgs0kgb2d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>e.g. prompted sabotage, spontaneously taking bad actions on alignment honeypots, eval gaming, training gaming, reward hacking etc. -- perhaps with the exception of some things like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me" rel="noreferrer"><span>slop</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn18ggwqsczvs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref18ggwqsczvs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Cases where models lack introspective awareness of the undesired thing are usually much harder to catch, e.g. isolated backdoors, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891" rel="noreferrer"><span>conditional misalignment</span></a><span>, or our most difficult </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07810" rel="noreferrer"><span>sandbagging model organisms</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqxaybamx5ar"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqxaybamx5ar">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>e.g. more comprehensive expansions of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.30036" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Aligned, Orthogonal or In-conflict</span></i></a><span> covering many more areas and pathways of potential degradation. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891" rel="noreferrer"><span>Conditional</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.18397" rel="noreferrer"><span>misalignment</span></a><span> / </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mqaig6ARtCo" rel="noreferrer"><span>whack-a-mole training</span></a><span> is also relevant. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpivk34b7kl9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpivk34b7kl9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This notion of oversight is weaker than </span><i><span>scalable oversight</span></i><span>, which asks whether oversight techniques can be made robust enough to train superhuman systems. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndhibdx10zv5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdhibdx10zv5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span>Likelihood </span></i><span>follows the </span><a href="https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/explaining-uncertainty-in-uk-intelligence-assessment/explaining-uncertainty-in-uk-intelligence-assessment" rel="noreferrer"><span>PHIA probability yardstick</span></a><span>, e.g. "</span><i><span>Likely</span></i><span>" indicates that we judge a 55-75% chance of this issue being the severity listed in the "severity" column before full automation of AI R&amp;D.</span></p><img alt="PHIA.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779275855/lexical_client_uploads/avuvuni5zem5pid1lxqi.png" /><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn97yltylehzd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref97yltylehzd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span>Severity</span></i><span> (pips in the rightmost column) refers to how badly that pathway would undermine the corresponding oversight channel if it did arise: ⬤ extremely high (would substantially compromise the underlying oversight channel with no clear fallback), 🔴 high, 🟡 medium, 🟢 low. Likelihood and severity ratings are judgements about the regime before full automation of AI R&amp;D, not about substantially superhuman systems.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmgj2c26oefj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmgj2c26oefj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div ### [AI #169: New Knowledge](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xWsBwrboYDEMdj8TC/ai-169-new-knowledge)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Even in a relatively quiet period, AI is out there creating new knowledge. The new knowledge in question is OpenAI getting us the first truly impressive math result that comes from an AI, a solution to the unit distance problem.</p>
<p>We’re about to learn a different kind of knowledge later today when the White House issues its executive order, or when the judges rule in Anthropic’s DC case.</p>
<p>And then there’s the other kind of new knowledge, which is the knowledge that things are fake slop, such as a particular formerly supposedly prestigious literary prize.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, METR issued a risk report on frontier models, concluding that they don’t yet have the means, motive and opportunity to cause the big issues, but that this would not obviously last so much longer.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25298"></span>


</div>
<p>Andrej Karpathy has joined Anthropic, explicitly to do recursive self-improvement. He plans to later return to his education work, but if he succeeds at his new task there might not be anything left to return to. Congratulations to both sides, but also yikes.</p>
<p>Elon Musk’s case against OpenAI has been dismissed, because he waited too long.</p>

<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2056064547421847579">They’ll be right back after GPT-5.5 takes this music break</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/gbrl_dick/status/2056636791805948394">AI text usually reflects consensus that is easily available</a>, which is one reason our eyes glaze over after seeing it. That helps explain why the AI text you request is interesting (you actually wanted the consensus answer) while that by others is terrible.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/WilliamAEden/status/2056952950652583976">Data science remains a weak point</a>. Detailed persnickety modeling and statistics are places the AIs often don’t understand what to do or why you need what you need.</p>

<h4>The Prior Restraint Era Begins</h4>


<p>Why do we get Gemini Flash 3.5 and not yet Pro 3.5? Here is one plausible theory.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2057194565795475855">Andrew Curran</a>: I want to make this prediction now so I can quote it later. Gemini Pro 3.5 and GPT-5.6 are both ready now, and both labs want to release them, but they are being held back for safety testing in a test flight of the new regulations in the forthcoming executive order.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2057202754503012564">Dean W. Ball</a>: Probably true. I think the reality is that we’re going to have a de facto licensing regime (“voluntary”), where the government will give green lights to the labs on releases. That is fine as a very temporary solution, but it’s opaque and essentially lawless. We will need to institutionalize this stuff and create transparent, objective, and predictable protocols to structure it.</p>
<p>I support having private bodies, overseen by government, having a major role in evaluating and setting technical standards, internal governance practices within labs, and similar. But more important than my specific idea is that we operate according to the rule of law, rather than create a de facto, opaque licensing regime in the name of maintaining the illusion that pre-deployment review is “voluntary” and that we are Not Regulating AI.</p></blockquote>
<p>I am guessing GPT-5.6 is not ready yet, but it is possible. There is also variance in what ‘ready’ means here. AI releases usually have a several week period in which the AI companies get them ready for release, including various forms of safety testing. The new government testing regime is not obviously slowing this down unless they find an active reason not to release.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/wp-intelligence/ai-tech-brief/2026/05/20/ai-tech-brief-googles-ai-operating-system/">The executive order is expected today.</a> Word is they have backed away from rules with formal teeth, and testing will be ‘voluntary,’ but the air quotes apply.</p>
