### [Fragments: May 14](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-05-14.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Last week I spent a day at The Orchard Retreat, hosted by <a href="https://www.mechanical-orchard.com/">Mechanical Orchard</a>. that brought together several people working in software development to talk about the profession’s future with the rise of agentic programming. The event was help under the <a href="https://www.chathamhouse.org/about-us/chatham-house-rule">Chatham House Rule</a>, so I can’t attribute the comments and stories I heard. (If anyone recognizes themselves, and would like attribution, let me know.) Here are a few tidbits that caught my notebook.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>One group developed a behavioral clone of GNU Cobol compiler in Rust. The result is 70K lines of Rust and was built in 3 days. This is yet another sign of the ability of LLMs to do a good job of porting existing code to a new platform. Good regression tests are extremely valuable here (and I don’t know how good GNU Cobol’s are). There’s also the possibility of building a test suite if you have access an existing implementation.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Large spec documents can be complex for a human to review. One attendee shared the idea of getting the LLM to interview a human expert, asking the human questions to verify the correctness of the specification, a form of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/InterrogatoryLLM.html">Interrogatory LLM</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Not specifically about AI - but I liked how one attendee commented that the first thing they do when consulting with an organization is to read the guidelines for their change-control board. This is the scar tissue of what’s gone wrong in the past. I’ve often said that to understand why a thing is the way it is, you need to understand the history of how it got there. This seems like an excellent way to tap into important parts of that history.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>My colleagues who work with modernizing legacy systems have long been rather sniffy about “Lift and Shift” - porting a legacy system to a new platform while retaining <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/patterns-legacy-displacement/feature-parity.html">Feature Parity</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>We see this pattern as a huge missed opportunity. Often the old systems have bloated over time, with many features unused by users (50% according to a 2014 Standish Group report) and business processes that have evolved over time. Replacing these features is a waste. Instead, try to muster the energy to take a step back and understand what users currently need, and prioritize these needs against business outcomes and metrics.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>But this point of view was developed before LLM’s ability to port code appeared. One attendee who does a lot of work in this field said they believed that lifting and shifting to a new platform should now be always the first step in a legacy migration. The cost is no longer as formidable as it used to be, and a better environment makes further evolution much cheaper. Just don’t stop there.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Several attendees were from the financial industry, and thus were immersed the problems of complex legacy environments coupled with regulatory controls and significant risk should software do something wrong with money. One of their issues is the complexity we run into when a financial product is offered in multiple jurisdictions, each with their own regulations to satisfy. There’s a lot of software complexity in deciding which jurisdiction applies, and picking the right set of rules at the right point of the workflow.</p>

<p>The question here is whether the rapidity of agentic programming means that we can build individual, simpler systems for each jurisdiction. We would then use LLMs to ensure consistency between them, so that as the product rules change, each system reflects that into its own environment.</p>

<p>A large part of software design is about identifying what is the same and what differs between various business contexts. Where things are the same, and need to be the same, we are rightly wary of duplicating code, since this increases the cost of updates the dangers of inconsistency. The interesting question is what role LLMs can play to give us new tools to tackle this.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>As is usually the case in gatherings like this, folks were concerned about junior developers. When we work with The Genie, our value comes from good judgment - how do we teach that? This group did have one common tool - <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/PairProgramming.html">Pair Programming</a>. One of the key benefits of pairing has always been skills transfer, and here an experienced agentic programmer can pass on their judgment for software design and how to use the genie to get there. And the junior will often have a trick or two to share too - that fresh pair of eyes in particularly valuable in the shift to our agentic future.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Historically, we use computer systems to bring order to chaotic human processes. Is AI reversing that?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>So much software is involved in data transformation. Those records over there need to be consumed by these APIs over here, but there are differences in how the data is structured, often due to being in different <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Contexts</a>, so we have to do some conversion. Agents are particularly adept at writing this kind of transformation code, which is often more tedious than we’d like.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Chaos Engineering has become a valuable technique to improve resiliency, made famous by Netflix’s Chaos Monkey that randomly breaks live services to see how well the ecosystem reacts and recovers. What would a Chaos Monkey for AI look like? Would it deliberately introduce hallucinations into a pipeline to see if sensors were able to catch them?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Back at my desk</p>

<p>There’s been a bunch of questions about the article on <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/">Structured-Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD)</a> that the authors answered in a <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/#faq">Q&amp;A section</a>. One in particular caught my eye:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Have you considered having an agent do the prompt/spec review itself — not a human reviewing the Canvas, but an agent that reads the REASONS Canvas alongside the code diff and verifies alignment?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The reply talks about how there is an available command to do this, but there downsides. In particular one reason not to do this automatically is:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Letting humans learn. Review is also where humans learn from the AI’s choices — patterns, trade-offs, options they had not thought of. Cutting humans out speeds things up, but it blocks the long-term skill growth that SPDD is designed to protect. […] Once enough decision rules build up to give us real confidence, we may shift more of the review to the agent step by step — but the part where humans learn from the AI is something we plan to keep.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>One of the ways we should judge the value of an AI tool is how much it helps us humans learn more about the world we inhabit and build.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In some strange way I injured my elbow last week. No idea how, there was no event where I said “oh shit”. It just gradually started hurting and swelling. My life-long strategy to avoid sports injuries<sup id="fnref:1"><a class="footnote" href="https://martinfowler.com/feed.atom#fn:1" rel="footnote">1</a></sup> had defied me. I applied ice and ibuprofin, the swelling went down, but my range of motion got worse. I’m glad I learned to use a knife and fork in English childhood, so I normally eat with my left hand.</p>

<p>I noticed that that loss of range of motion occurred after I got home, when I started spending all day at the computer again. I might not use my elbow directly, but my right hand does a lot of typing and mousing. My desk set up is pretty ergonomic, with a <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/kinesis-advantage2.html">good keyboard</a>, a wrist rest for the mouse, and arm rests on my chair. But even so, did my computer use make my elbow get worse once I got home? I can’t imagine not using the computer, for me writing has become an unstoppable habit. But maybe I should use this opportunity to explore voice input - after all most people can speak faster than they can type.</p>

<p>I tried this many years ago, when a colleague told me how good voice recognition was once it trained to you. I tried it, and indeed the voice recognition, even in those pre-AI days, was very good. But it didn’t work for me. When I’m writing I rapidly type words into Emacs, but almost immediately I go back to edit them. Write two sentences, edit them, write another, re-edit the paragraph. The back-and-forth between seeing my words and thinking about them is tight - I can’t just dictate my words.</p>

<p>That made me reflect further. I only started using a computer for my writing in my 20s. At school I had to write longhand, and in university to type on typewriter. But those media don’t support the constant rewriting that I do now. Would I even have become a writer had the text editor not been invented?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>James Pritchard thinks that many developers are over-using agents at run-time in their products, when <a href="https://james-pritchard.com/blog/llms-are-functions">LLMs are better used as functions</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The problem with agents isn’t that they don’t work. It’s that they work unpredictably. You trade a known execution path for “autonomy” that mostly means “I don’t know what it’s going to do.” When an agent-powered feature breaks in production, you’re debugging a conversation transcript, not a stack trace.</p>

  <p>Most “agent” use cases are actually workflows, a known sequence of steps where one or two of those steps happen to involve an LLM. You don’t need autonomy for that. You need a function call.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that functions compose predictably, so if you know the workflow, then composing in a program text is better than agents figuring out how to coordinate themselves. It’s  faster, and needs less tokens. It’s usually easier to deal with failures, since the scope of the interaction is smaller.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Pritchard also thinks that people <a href="https://james-pritchard.com/blog/skills-junk-drawer">use skills far more than they should</a>. He thinks people accumulate folders of markdown skill files but LLMs use them inconsistently, often missing them when they’re needed, or bloating context when they are not. Many things that should go in skills should be other parts of a <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">harness</a>, preferably computational. Skills should only be used with deliberate, infrequent workflows.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The skills obsession is a symptom of a deeper pattern: people reaching for configuration when they should be reaching for architecture.</p>

  <p>“The LLM doesn’t write good tests.” Don’t write a testing skill. Are your existing tests inconsistent? Is the test setup complex? Fix those things and it’ll write good tests without being told how. Point it at a test file you’re proud of. Code is clearer than English.</p>

  <p>[…]</p>

  <p>The best setup is one where you barely need to configure the LLM at all. A clean codebase with clear patterns, a short project config for the non-obvious stuff, hooks for automation, and maybe one or two skills for specific workflows you run intentionally. That’s it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>An oft-stated point about the rise of agentic programming is that we have to start dealing with non-determinism in our work. Of course that’s somewhat of a simplification, because some aspects of software development have long had to face non-determinism. A notable example of this is distributed systems, and a notable figure in helping us probe the truly uncomfortable waters of distributed systems is Kyle Kingsbury (Aphyr).</p>

<p>Last month he dropped a long article (the pdf is 32 pages) on how he sees our LLM-enabled future. The title “<a href="https://aphyr.com/posts/411-the-future-of-everything-is-lies-i-guess">The Future of Everything is Lies, I Guess</a>” betrays his lack of enthusiasm for this future.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Some readers are undoubtedly upset that I have not devoted more space to the wonders of machine learning—how amazing LLMs are at code generation, how incredible it is that Suno can turn hummed melodies into polished songs. But this is not an article about how fast or convenient it is to drive a car. We all know cars are fast. I am trying to ask what will happen to the shape of cities.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>It’s worth the long read, even if it isn’t terribly cheerful. Kingsbury brings up many of worries about AI’s growth from the perspective of someone who is clearly well-informed about their capabilities.</p>

<p>His view is that the best response to all this is that we should stop. He wants to avoid using AIs for his writing, software, or personal life. He thinks those working for the AI companies should quit. And yet he also knows that these tools are useful, and wants to use them.</p>

<p>I’m both a hoper and a doomer when it comes to our AI future. Fundamentally I see any powerful technology as a big bus: we are either on it, or get run over by it. I’m onboard the bus because I don’t think putting up some barriers would stop me being crushed by its wheels. Maybe if I’m on the bus I can join some people to influence the driver a bit. I’m also very reluctant to speculate on the future outcomes of anything, let alone something as powerful as this. Did the early industrialists in the late eighteenth century have any clue what the industrial revolution they unleashed would do? While it created many harms, it also created a massive rise in the living standards of millions of people, at least those whose countries were on the bus. AI may create benefits that I can’t really dream of, although I can glimpse it when it helps <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/my-80th-birthday-i-bought-myself-new-brain-jim-highsmith-8qcrc/">a friend stave off Parkinson’s disease.</a></p>

<p>Those hopes are there, but Kingsbury’s article shines a light on the darker elements of the here-and-now, asking serious questions of responsibility</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>a part of my work as a moderator of a Mastodon instance is to respond to user reports, and occasionally those reports are for CSAM, and I am legally obligated to review and submit that content to the NCMEC. I do not want to see these images, and I really wish I could unsee them. On dark mornings, when I sit down at my computer and find a moderation report for AI-generated images of sexual assault, I sometimes wish that the engineers working at OpenAI etc. had to see these images too. Perhaps it would make them reflect on the technology they are ushering into the world, and how “alignment” is working out in practice.</p>
</blockquote>

<div class="footnotes">
  <ol>
    <li id="fn:1">
      <p>Don’t do sports <a class="reversefootnote" href="https://martinfowler.com/feed.atom#fnref:1">↩</a></p>
    </li>
  </ol>
</div>

---

### [Bliki: Interrogatory LLM](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/InterrogatoryLLM.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we need an LLM to perform a complex task, we often need to feed it a
  lot of context. Coming up with a design for a new feature requires
  descriptions of how we want the feature to appear to the user, guidelines on
  how it should be implemented, information on external systems to consult, and
  so on. All this can be several pages of markdown. The obvious way to do
  this is for a human to write this context, but an alternative is to use an LLM to write
  this context after interviewing a human.</p>

<p>The way I can do this is to prompt the LLM to interrogate me. It should ask me
  all the questions it needs to create this appropriate context. I can feed much
  of the information it needs, and tell it other sources it needs to consult if
  it can't figure those out itself. Once it's done, it can then create the
  context report for another session (perhaps with another model) to carry out
  the next step.</p>

<p>I first saw a decent description of this approach in <a href="https://harper.blog/2025/02/16/my-llm-codegen-workflow-atm/">Harper
  Reed's blog</a>. A striking element of his approach is insisting that the LLM
  ask only one question at a time. (When I tried it, I found it needed to be
  frequently reminded of this.)</p>

<p>Another way to use an interrogatory LLM is to give it a document, such as a
  software specification, that captures knowledge about a domain - and then ask
  the LLM to interview a human expert to determine if the document is accurate.
  This is an alternative to getting the human expert to read the document to
  review it. People often find reviewing hard, so a conversation with an LLM
  might be more fruitful, particularly if the document isn't well-written.</p>

<p>Naturally we can use both of these, using one interrogatory LLM to build a
  document, then using other interrogatory LLMs to review it with other experts.
  </p>

<p>The above is getting an LLM to create or assess context for a particular
  use of an LLM. But the technique is more broadly applicable. I've become a
  natural writer, someone who finds the process of writing an essential part of
  thinking. To really understand something, I need to write about it. But
  different people are different. Many folks find writing hard, often
  <i>very</i> hard. This can be a real problem when we need to get information
  out of someone's head into a form that other humans can consume. Maybe such
  people would find it easier to ask an LLM to interview them than to write a
  document themselves. Certainly the result will have that tang of AI-writing
  that folks like me shudder at - but that's better than not having the
  information itself, either due to rushed writing or no writing at all. </p>

---

### [What is Code](https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Increasingly humans delegate writing code to agents. Will there even be
      source code in the future? To wrestle with this question, we have to
      understand what code is. <b class="author">Unmesh Joshi</b> sees code as having
      two distinct but intertwined purposes: <i>instructions to a machine</i> and a
      <i>conceptual model of the problem domain</i>. He explores why it's vital
      to build a vocabulary to talk to the machine, how programming languages
      are thinking tools, and how this affects our future as we work with LLMs.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: May  5](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-05-05.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Over the last couple of months Rahul Garg published a series of posts here on how to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/">reduce the friction in AI-assisted programming</a>. To make it easier to put these ideas into practice he’s now built an <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/#ThesePatternsAsInstallableInfrastructure">open-source framework to operationalize these patterns</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>AI coding assistants jump straight to code, silently make design decisions, forget constraints mid-conversation, and produce output nobody reviewed against real engineering standards. Lattice fixes this with composable skills in three tiers – atoms, molecules, refiners – that embed battle-tested engineering disciplines (Clean Architecture, DDD, design-first methodology, secure coding, and more), plus a living context layer (the .lattice/ folder) that accumulates your project’s standards, decisions, and review insights. The system gets smarter with use – after a few feature cycles, atoms aren’t applying generic rules, they’re applying your rules, informed by your history.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>It can be installed as a Claude Code plugin or downloaded for use with any AI tool.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>This is also a good point to note that the article by my colleagues Wei Zhang and Jessie Jie Xia on <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/">Structured-Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD)</a> has generated an enormous amount of traffic, and quite a few questions. They have thus added a <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/#faq">Q&amp;A section</a> to the article that answers a dozen of them.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jessica Kerr (Jessitron) posted a merry tidbit of building a tool to work with conversation logs. <a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/04/27/communication-is-hard-but-sometimes-i-can-fix-it/">She observes the double feedback loop</a> involved.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There are (at least) two feedback loops running here. One is the development loop, with Claude doing what I ask and then me checking whether that is indeed what I want.[…] Then there’s a meta-level feedback loop, the “is this working?” check when I feel resistance. Frustration, tedium, annoyance–these feelings are a signal to me that maybe this work could be easier.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The double loop here is both changing the thing we are building but also changing the thing we are using to build the thing we are building.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>As developers using software to build software, we have potential to mold our own work environment. With AI making software change superfast, changing our program to make debugging easier pays off immediately. Also, this is fun!</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Indeed it is, and makes me think that agents are allowing us to (re)discover one of the Great Lost Joys of software development - that of molding my development environment to exactly fit the problem and my personal tastes. A while ago I wrote about this under the name <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/InternalReprogrammability.html">Internal Reprogramability</a>. It was a central feature of the Smalltalk and Lisp communities but was mostly lost as we got complex and polished IDEs (although the unix command line gives a hint of its appeal).</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ashley MacIsaac is a musician from Cape Breton, who plays folk-influenced music (I have a couple of his albums in my collection). Google generated an AI overview that asserted that had been convicted of crimes, including sexual assault and was on the national sex-offender registry. These were completely false, confusing him with another man with the same name. MacIsaac is <a href="https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/nova-scotia/cape-breton-fiddler-ashley-macisaac-lawsuit-against-google-9.7187490">suing Google for defamation</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>“This was not a search engine just scanning through things and giving somebody else’s story […] It was published by them. And to me, that is defamation. The guardrails were not there to prevent Google AI from publishing that content.”</p>
</blockquote>

<p>MacIsaac’s point is that Google must take responsibility for what a tool it controls publishes. MacIsaac suffered genuine harm here, not just to his reputation, but he also had a concert canceled and the claims affect his performing.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>“I felt that tangible fear from something that was published by a media company,” he said in an interview with The Canadian Press. “I feared for my own safety going on stage because of what I was labelled as. And I don’t know how long this will follow me.”</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Too often tech companies try to dodge the consequences of their actions. There are genuine issues about the difficulties of monitoring what’s published at scale, but that’s a responsibility that they should face up to.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Stephen O’Grady (RedMonk) takes a serious look at <a href="https://redmonk.com/sogrady/2026/04/29/infrastructure-spend-in-the-ai-era/">how much the big tech companies are spending on AI build-outs</a>. The sums involved are staggering, not just in absolute terms (over $100 Billion), but also compared to the revenues of the companies involved. Firms like Amazon, Alphabet, and Microsoft are spending over 50% of their revenues (not profits).  Meta and Oracle hit or pass 75% of revenues.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>That level of investment would have been unthinkable a decade ago. Today, the chart suggests it’s table stakes</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There is a notable exception: Apple. Here they are clearly Thinking Different, eyeballing the chart they seem closer to 10% of revenues.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Most folks I talk to about agentic programming are using models in the cloud: Claude, Codex, and the like. Everyone agrees these are the most powerful models, the ones that triggered the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/NovemberInflection.html">November Inflection</a>. But do we need to use the Most Powerful Models, particularly when we have to ship data to them, and pay handsomely for the privilege? Willem van den Ende <a href="https://willemvandenende.com/blog/engineering/my-local-agentic-dev-setup-today">considers an alternative</a>, that local models are Good Enough.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Assumptions<br />
- We are all figuring this out.<br />
- Quality of a harness (coding agent + “skills” + extensions) can matter as least as much as the model<br />
- Running open models and an open coding agent + custom extensions takes time, but pays off in understanding and a stable base where engineering effort compounds<br />
- Open, local, models have (for me) crossed the point where they are good enough for daily work with a coding agent.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The post describes in detail his setup for local model work. It includes sandboxing with Nono, which is something to consider even if using a Cloud model - such powerful tools need a <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar/techniques/zero-trust-architecture">Zero Trust Architecture</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In case you haven’t noticed, those last two fragments resonate. Apple isn’t playing the cloud AI model game, they are saving a huge amount of money, and if local models end up being the future, they’ll be looking rather wise. Van den Ende’s post led me to a podcast by Nate B Jones that argues that <a href="https://natesnewsletter.substack.com/p/executive-briefing-the-ai-race-youre">Apple is replaying a fifty-year old strategy</a> here. All those years ago anyone who used a computer bought time on a mainframe, the Apple II put far less capable compute into the home and small office. From there came spreadsheets, desktop publishing, and the modern home computer - things that weren’t possible using mainframes.</p>

<p>He sees the rise of John Ternus as CEO isn’t merely a switch to a known insider successor - but a bet that the future of AI is sophisticated hardware in the home, office, and pocket. If Open Source models are Good Enough, then why spend money sending tokens - containing your sensitive data - to the AI megacorps?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Talking of five decades in the past, it was in 1974 that Fred Brooks opened <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/MythicalManMonth.html">one of the most influential books</a> in our profession with these paragraphs:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>No scene from prehistory is quite so vivid as that of the mortal struggles of great beasts in the tar pits. In the mind’s eye one sees dinosaurs, mammoths, and sabertoothed tigers struggling against the grip of the tar. The fiercer the struggle, the more entangling the tar, and no beast is so strong or so skillful but that he ultimately sinks.</p>

  <p>Large-system programming has over the past decade been such a tar pit, and many great and powerful beasts have thrashed violently in it. Most have emerged with running systems — few have met goals, schedules, and budgets. Large and small, massive or wiry, team after team has become entangled in the tar. No one thing seems to cause the difficulty — any particular paw can be pulled away. But the accumulation of simultaneous and interacting factors brings slower and slower motion. Everyone seems to have been surprised by the stickiness of the problem, and it is hard to discern the nature of it. But we must try to understand it if we are to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>With the title of his recent post Kent Beck summons up that imagery as the <a href="https://tidyfirst.substack.com/p/genie-tarpit">Genie Tarpit</a>. After explaining why skilled software development is about building both features and futures, he observes that these AI tools aren’t doing a good job of producing software with the kind of internal quality that is needed for a good future.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Here’s what I’ve observed — genies naturally live down &amp; to the left of muddling. The “plausible deniability” task orientation of the genie leaves it claiming success even though the code doesn’t work at all. And complexity piles on complexity until even the genie can’t pretend to make progress any more.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>It’s still an open question whether, or to what extent, internal quality matters in the age of agentic programming. One view is, as Laura Tacho puts it, “The Venn Diagram of Developer Experience and Agent Experience is a circle”. Well organized elements, with good naming, help The Genie understand code, so are important if we can continue to go beyond small disposable systems. The other view is that such internal quality doesn’t matter, that the galaxy brain of LLMs will make sense of the biggest bowls of spaghetti. Maybe not now, but after a couple more inflections.</p>

<p>That’s the fundamental question. Can The Genie evade the tar pit, or will it struggle fruitlessly against the tar’s sticky grip?</p>

---

### [Bliki: Mythical Man Month](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/MythicalManMonth.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In the early 1960s, Fred Brooks managed the development of IBM's System/360
  computer systems. After it was done he penned his thoughts in the book <a href="https://www.amazon.com/gp/product/0201835959/ref=as_li_tl?ie=UTF8&amp;camp=1789&amp;creative=9325&amp;creativeASIN=0201835959&amp;linkCode=as2&amp;tag=martinfowlerc-20">The
  Mythical Man-Month</a> which became one of the most influential books on software
  development after its publication in 1975. Reading it in 2026, we'll find some
  of it outdated, but it also retains many lessons that are still relevant today.</p>

<p>The book contains Brooks's law: “Adding manpower to a late software project
  makes it later.” The issue here is communication, as the number of people
  grows, the number of communication paths between those people grows
  exponentially. Unless these paths are skillfully designed, then work quickly
  falls apart.</p>

<p>Perhaps my most enduring lesson from this book is the importance of
  <b>conceptual integrity</b></p>

<blockquote>
<p>I will contend that conceptual integrity is the most important
    consideration in system design. It is better to have a system omit certain
    anomalous features and improvements, but to reflect one set of design ideas,
    than to have one that contains many good but independent and uncoordinated
    ideas.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He argues that conceptual integrity comes from both simplicity and
  straightforwardness - the latter being how easily we can compose elements.
  This point of view has been a strong influence upon my career, the pursuit
  of conceptual integrity underpins much of my work.</p>

<p>The <a href="https://www.amazon.com/gp/product/0201835959/ref=as_li_tl?ie=UTF8&amp;camp=1789&amp;creative=9325&amp;creativeASIN=0201835959&amp;linkCode=as2&amp;tag=martinfowlerc-20">anniversary edition</a> of this book is the one to get, because it also
  includes his even-more influential 1986 essay “No Silver Bullet”.</p>

---

### [Fragments: April 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Chris Parsons has updated <a href="https://www.chrismdp.com/coding-with-ai/">his guide on using AI to code</a>. This is his third update, what I like about it is that he gives a lot of concrete information about how he uses AI, with sufficient detail that we can learn from him. His advice also resonates with the better advice I’ve seen out there, so the article makes a good overview of the state of using AI for software development.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I wrote the previous version of this post in March 2025, updated it once in August, and it has been linked from almost everything I have written about AI engineering since. The fundamentals from that post still hold: keep changes small, build guardrails, document ruthlessly, and make sure every change gets verified before it ships. One thing has had to move with the volume. “Verified” used to mean “read by you”. With modern agent throughput, it has to mean “checked by tests, by type checkers, by automated gates, or by you where your judgement matters”. The check still happens; it just does not always happen in your head.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like Simon Willison, he makes a clear distinction between vibe coding, where you don’t look at or care about the code, and agentic engineering. He recommends either Claude Code or Codex CLI. He considers the inner harness provided by his preferred tools to be a key part of their advantage.</p>

<p>He sees verification is the key thing to focus on:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A team that can generate five approaches and verify all five in an afternoon will outpace a team that generates one and waits a week for feedback. The game is not “how fast can we build” any more. It is “how fast can we tell whether this is right”. That shifts where to invest. Build better review surfaces, not better prompts. Make feedback unnecessary where you can by having the agent verify against a realistic environment before it asks a human, and make feedback instant where you cannot.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The key role of the programmer is in training the AI to write software properly, and the most important thing skilled agentic programmers can do is pass that skill onto other developers.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And if you are a senior engineer worried that your job is quietly turning into approving diffs: it is. The way out is to train the AI so the diffs are right the first time, to make yourself the person on the team who shapes the harness, and to make that work the visible thing you are measured on. That role compounds in a way that reviewing never will.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Early this month Birgitta Böckeler wrote a superb <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">article on Harness Engineering</a>. (That’s not just my opinion, judging by the crazy traffic it’s attracted.) Birgitta has now recorded a video <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uLWOLmeHOSE">discussion with Chris Ford on Harness Engineering</a>, which is well worth a watch.</p>

<p>In it they focus on discussing the role of computational sensors in the harness, such as static analysis and tests.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>LLMs are great for exploratory and fuzzy rules, but once you have something that really is objective, converting it to a formal, unambiguous, deterministic format can give you more assurance</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Birgitta did some experiments to explore the benefits of adding sensors, including a deep dive on using static analysis. She found it’s more useful as agents can really address <em>every</em> warning, and don’t slack off like humans do.</p>

<!-- 
got to 44 minutes
-->
<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Adam Tornhill considers an age-old question: <a href="https://adamtornhill.substack.com/p/how-long-should-a-function-be-and">how long should a function be?</a> This question is still relevent in the age of agentic programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>AI models do not “understand” code the way humans do. They infer meaning from patterns in tokens and depend heavily on what is explicitly expressed in the code.</p>

  <p>Research shows that naming plays a critical role. When meaningful identifiers are replaced with arbitrary names, model performance drops significantly. Current models rely heavily on literal features—names, structure, and local context—rather than inferred semantics.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like me, he doesn’t think the answer is to think about how many lines should be in a function, instead it’s all about providing better structure. He has a good example of how a well-chosen function defines useful concepts, where a function wraps four lines of code, returning a new concept that enters the vocabulary of the program.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Functions are the first unit of structure in a codebase. They define how logic is grouped, how intent is communicated, and how change is localized. If the function boundaries are wrong, everything built on top of them becomes harder to understand and harder to evolve.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This fits with my writing that the key to function length is the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FunctionLength.html">separation between intention and implementation</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you have to spend effort into looking at a fragment of code to figure out what it’s doing, then you should extract it into a function and name the function after that “what”. That way when you read it again, the purpose of the function leaps right out at you, and most of the time you won’t need to care about how the function fulfills its purpose - which is the body of the function.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many folks in my feeds recommended Nilay Patel’s post on <a href="https://www.theverge.com/podcast/917029/software-brain-ai-backlash-databases-automation">Why People Hate AI</a>. He thinks that many people in the software world have “software brain”:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The simplest definition I’ve come up with is that it’s when you see the whole world as a series of databases that can be controlled with the structured language of software code. Like I said, this is a powerful way of seeing things. So much of our lives run through databases, and a bunch of important companies have been built around maintaining those databases and providing access to them.</p>

  <p>Zillow is a database of houses. Uber is a database of cars and riders. YouTube is a database of videos. The Verge’s website is a database of stories. You can go on and on and on. Once you start seeing the world as a bunch of databases, it’s a small jump to feeling like you can control everything if you can just control the data.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Software Brain views people into databases, and oddly enough, a lot of people don’t like that. Which is why so many polls reveal the negative feelings folks have about the AI movement.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Even taking the time to consider how much of your life is captured in databases makes people unhappy. No one wants to be surveilled constantly, and especially not in a way that makes tech companies even more powerful. But getting everything in a database so software can see it is a preoccupation of the AI industry. It’s why all the meeting systems have AI note takers in them now.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Patel draws a similarity that I’ve often made - that between programmers and lawyers. Lawyers who draw up contracts are creating a protocol for how the parties in the contract should behave. As Patel puts it:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If the heart of software brain is the idea that thinking in the structured language of code can make things happen in the real world, well, the heart of lawyer brain is that thinking in the structured legal language of statutes and citations can also make things happen. Hell, it can give you power over society.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The difference, of course, is that law is non-deterministic. Litigation is resolving what happens when people have different ideas about how those contracts should execute.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was chatting recently with a company who wanted to use AI to make sense of their internal data. The potential was great, but the problem was that the data a mess. People put stuff into fields that didn’t make sense, and there was little consistency about how people classified important entities. As someone commented</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>the hardest problem with internal data is precise, consistent definitions</p>
</blockquote>

<p>You can imagine my astonishment. (i.e. none at all - this has been a constant theme during all my decades with computers.) The difficulty of getting such definitions undermines much of the hopes of Software Brain</p>

<p>This resonates with our relationship with LLMs when programming. Precise and consistent definitions strike me as crucial to effective communication with The Genie. These definitions need to grow in the conversation, and be tended over time. Conceptual modeling will be a key skill for agentic programming and whatever comes next. (At least I hope it will, since it’s a part of programming I really enjoy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Patel’s article refers to Ezra Klein’s post about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/03/29/opinion/ai-claude-chatgpt-gemini-mcluhan.html?unlocked_article_code=1.eFA.abX-.lGEOqsmKZVY_&amp;smid=url-share">new feeling in San Francisco</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>You might think that A.I. types in Silicon Valley, flush with cash, are on top of the world right now. I found them notably insecure. They think the A.I. age has arrived and its winners and losers will be determined, in part, by speed of adoption. The argument is simple enough: The advantages of working atop an army of A.I. assistants and coders will compound over time, and to begin that process now is to launch yourself far ahead of your competition later. And so they are racing one another to fully integrate A.I. into their lives and into their companies. But that doesn’t just mean using A.I. It means making themselves legible to the A.I.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That legibility is the heart of Patel’s observation. That’s why I see many colleagues of mine dumping all their email, meeting notes, slide decks and everything else into files that AI can read and work with. This works to the strengths of AI, we know that AI is really good at querying unstructured information. So I can figure out what’s buried in my notes in a way that’s far more effective than hoping I’m typing the right search regex.</p>

<p>I’ve been using Gemini a fair bit for exactly this on the web, finding it easier to write a question to it than to throw search terms at Google. Gemini keeps a record of my past requests, and uses that to help it tune what I’m looking for. As Klein observes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[The AI] is constantly referring back to other things it knows, or thinks it knows, about me. Sycophancy, in my experience, has given way to an occasionally unsettling attentiveness; a constant drawing of connections between my current concerns and my past queries, like a therapist desperate to prove he’s been paying close attention.</p>

  <p>The result is a strange amalgam of feeling seen and feeling caricatured.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like myself, Klein is a writer, and is faced by the same temptation that I have when I think about AI and writing. Maybe instead of toiling over articles, I should ask an LLM to create an <code>AGENTS.md</code> file that summarizes my writing style, and every few days ask it to compose an article on some subject, read it, tweak it, and then publish my erudite musings. But that’s not at all appealing to me. I want understanding to grow in <em>my brain</em>, not the LLM’s transient session. Writing to explain my thinking to others is how I refine that thinking, “chiseling that idea into something publishable” as Klein puts it. To have an AI write for me is to cripple my own mind.</p>

---

### [Structured-Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD)](https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven//card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>LLM programming assistants have demonstrated considerable value, but
      mostly with individual developers. The internal IT organization in
      Thoughtworks has been using them for their teams and have developed a
      method and workflow called Structured Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD).
      <b class="author">Wei Zhang and Jessie Jie Xia</b> describe a simple example of
      this workflow with details in github. This workflow treats the prompts as
      a first-class artifact, kept with the code in version control, and used to
      align development with business needs. They have found that developers
      need three key skills to be effective: alignment, abstraction-first, and
      iterative review.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/">more…</a></p>

## LLM Infrastructure

### [v0.21.1rc0: [ROCm][CI] Stage B gating (#42025)](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.21.1rc0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <p>Signed-off-by: Andreas Karatzas <a href="mailto:akaratza@amd.com">akaratza@amd.com</a></p>

---

### [v0.21.0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.21.0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h2>Highlights</h2>
<p>This release features 367 commits from 202 contributors (49 new)!</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Transformers v4 deprecated</strong>: This release formally deprecates <code>transformers</code> v4 support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40389">#40389</a>). Users should migrate to <code>transformers</code> v5.</li>
<li><strong>C++20 build requirement</strong>: vLLM now requires a C++20-compatible compiler for compatibility with PyTorch (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40380">#40380</a>). This is a <strong>breaking build change</strong>.</li>
<li><strong>KV Offload + Hybrid Memory Allocator (HMA)</strong>: The KV offloading subsystem now integrates with the Hybrid Memory Allocator, including scheduler-side sliding window group support and full HMA enablement (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41228">#41228</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41445">#41445</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39571">#39571</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Speculative decoding with thinking budget</strong>: Speculative decoding now respects reasoning/thinking budgets, enabling correct spec decode for reasoning models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34668">#34668</a>).</li>
<li><strong>TOKENSPEED_MLA backend on Blackwell</strong>: A new TOKENSPEED_MLA attention backend is available for DeepSeek-R1/Kimi-K25 prefill + decode on Blackwell GPUs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41778">#41778</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Model Support</h3>
<ul>
<li>New architectures: MiMo-V2.5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40967">#40967</a>), Laguna XS.2 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41129">#41129</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41880">#41880</a>), Moondream3 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32325">#32325</a>), Qianfan-OCR (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40136">#40136</a>), Cohere MoE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40817">#40817</a>), Cohere Eagle (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42078">#42078</a>).</li>
<li>Speculative decoding: EAGLE for Mistral (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41024">#41024</a>), Gemma4 MTP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41745">#41745</a>), MTP for MiMo-V2.5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41905">#41905</a>), Cohere Eagle (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42078">#42078</a>).</li>
<li>DeepSeek V4: AMD/ROCm support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40871">#40871</a>), pipeline parallelism (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41694">#41694</a>), <code>max</code> reasoning effort (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40982">#40982</a>), disaggregated serving fixes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41957">#41957</a>).</li>
<li>Tool calling: Cohere reasoning and tool parsers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40422">#40422</a>), LFM2/2.5 tool parser (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39243">#39243</a>).</li>
<li>Gemma3/Gemma4: <code>hidden_act</code> variant support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40588">#40588</a>), pipeline parallelism fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40786">#40786</a>), MoE fixes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41206">#41206</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41574">#41574</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41401">#41401</a>), tool parser crash fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41991">#41991</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42188">#42188</a>).</li>
<li>Model Runner V2: Qwen3.5/Mamba hybrid model support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35520">#35520</a>), <code>logprob_token_ids</code> support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40559">#40559</a>).</li>
<li>CUDA graph: ViT CUDA graph support for Qwen2.5-VL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40830">#40830</a>).</li>
<li>Compatibility: Vendor HCXVisionConfig for Transformers v5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38447">#38447</a>), legacy <code>rope_type</code> checkpoint support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41734">#41734</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Engine Core</h3>
<ul>
<li>KV offloading + HMA: Scheduler-side sliding window groups (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41228">#41228</a>), full HMA enablement (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41445">#41445</a>), multi-connector HMA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39571">#39571</a>), per-job store completion (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39186">#39186</a>), DCP/PCP support in OffloadingConnector (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41549">#41549</a>), MooncakeStoreConnector for distributed KV offloading (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40900">#40900</a>).</li>
<li>Speculative decoding: Thinking budget support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34668">#34668</a>), independent drafter attention backend selection (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39930">#39930</a>), multimodal model support with warning (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41752">#41752</a>), per-step allocation elimination (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41043">#41043</a>).</li>
<li>Model Runner V2: Rejection sampling acceptance rate fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40651">#40651</a>), skip metadata rebuild before draft prefill (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40410">#40410</a>), rebuild metadata between draft decode steps (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41162">#41162</a>), Qwen3.5/Mamba hybrid support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35520">#35520</a>).</li>
<li>Routing: Replace routing replay with device cache and async D2H pipeline (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39917">#39917</a>).</li>
<li>Ray: RayExecutorV2 enabled by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41421">#41421</a>), actor name collision fix for DP &gt; 1 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40398">#40398</a>).</li>
<li>Stability: Two-phase pause to prevent scheduler deadlock (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39366">#39366</a>), thread-safe HF tokenizer wrappers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41181">#41181</a>), OOM prevention via <code>max_split_size_mb</code> during model loading (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41268">#41268</a>).</li>
<li>IndexCache support for DSA models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37735">#37735</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Hardware &amp; Performance</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>NVIDIA Blackwell</strong>: TOKENSPEED_MLA backend for DSR1/Kimi-K25 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41778">#41778</a>), faster per-token FP8 group quant packed kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41326">#41326</a>), FP8 on NVIDIA Thor/SM110 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39712">#39712</a>), CUTLASS scaled mm for non-compatible sizes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41868">#41868</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Performance</strong>: FlashInfer top-k/top-p sampler enabled by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40376">#40376</a>), FP8 FlashInfer attention for ViT (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38065">#38065</a>), TurboQuant shared dequant buffers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40941">#40941</a>), <code>AllPool.forward</code> 51% faster (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41163">#41163</a>), GPU&lt;-&gt;CPU sync elimination in pooling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41433">#41433</a>) and attention (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41434">#41434</a>), numpy zero-copy embedding serialization (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41681">#41681</a>), multimodal processor skip for text-only (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41246">#41246</a>), FlashInfer FP8 async TP fusion (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39505">#39505</a>), NVFP4 all-gather GEMM fusion for AsyncTP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41882">#41882</a>), re-enable allreduce+RMS fusion for DP/PP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41458">#41458</a>), DeepSeek bf16→fp32 via <code>torch.mm</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41300">#41300</a>), persistent MLA for sparse backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41990">#41990</a>), configurable safetensors checkpoint prefetch (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41499">#41499</a>), fused mhc_post_pre kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41536">#41536</a>), 2D-grid W8W8 group quant kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42153">#42153</a>), relaxed memory ordering for KV cache swaps (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39306">#39306</a>).</li>
<li><strong>AMD ROCm</strong>: ROCm 7.2.2 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41386">#41386</a>), DBO (Dynamic Batch Optimization) (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34726">#34726</a>), AITER Fused Allreduce+RMSNorm (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37646">#37646</a>), Fused Shared Expert (FSE) for Qwen3-Next (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39280">#39280</a>), DeepSeek V3.2 TP4 AITER MLA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41835">#41835</a>), GDN linear attention fusion (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40711">#40711</a>), eliminate redundant MoE buffer copies in AITER (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41713">#41713</a>), CPU offloading support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40549">#40549</a>), DeepEP API update (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39721">#39721</a>), cap Triton paged attention block size to fix shared memory OOM (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38502">#38502</a>).</li>
<li><strong>CPU</strong>: FP8 attention for AMX/AVX-512 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39445">#39445</a>), FP8 W8A16 linear (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41186">#41186</a>), FP8 W8A16 MoE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41314">#41314</a>), DNNL AVX2 W8A8 Int8 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41318">#41318</a>), Gated DeltaNet Attention for Qwen 3.5/3.6 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41025">#41025</a>), RISC-V OMP thread auto-binding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40569">#40569</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Intel XPU</strong>: Top-k/top-p sample kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39285">#39285</a>), out-of-place all-reduce (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41808">#41808</a>), LoRA support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38206">#38206</a>).</li>
<li><strong>IBM Power</strong>: VSX attention backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40451">#40451</a>).</li>
<li><strong>FlexAttention</strong>: Re-enabled for batch invariant mode (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40842">#40842</a>).</li>
<li><strong>MLA</strong>: Abstracted MLA prefill backends, eliminated cuDNN dependency (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32623">#32623</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Large Scale Serving</h3>
<ul>
<li>Disaggregated serving: Bi-directional KV cache transfers between P and D (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32553">#32553</a>), NIXL transfer redesign (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40731">#40731</a>), EPLB memory overhead optimization (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40013">#40013</a>), NIXL connector bumped to 1.x (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42364">#42364</a>), Mooncake KVConnectorStats for transfer observability (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40414">#40414</a>), NIXL P-node pre-admission rejection notification (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41269">#41269</a>), KV block release for skipped P-ranks (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40449">#40449</a>).</li>
<li>DCP: Pack output and LSE in DCP A2A (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41160">#41160</a>).</li>
<li>MoE: PluggableLayer interface for out-of-tree MoE runners (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35178">#35178</a>).</li>
<li>LoRA: Initial expert parallel (EP) support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40867">#40867</a>), Qwen3.5 LoRA fusion fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37912">#37912</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Quantization</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>NVFP4</strong>: KV cache support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40177">#40177</a>), Triton dequant/QDQ emulation kernels for Hopper and AMD (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40033">#40033</a>), GELU on TRT-LLM NvFP4 fused MoE for Gemma4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41050">#41050</a>), ModelOpt NVFP4 W4A16 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41769">#41769</a>), NVFP4 all-gather GEMM fusion for AsyncTP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41882">#41882</a>), GLM4-MoE NVFP4 loading fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41755">#41755</a>).</li>
<li><strong>MXFP4</strong>: Humming MXFP4 MoE backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41083">#41083</a>), FlashInfer CUTLASS MXFP4-MXFP8 MoE fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42089">#42089</a>).</li>
<li><strong>TurboQuant</strong>: Hybrid model and uniform quantization support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39931">#39931</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Compressed tensors</strong>: Allow configs with non-explicit ignores (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41965">#41965</a>).</li>
<li><strong>FP8</strong>: Bias loading fix (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41424">#41424</a>), FlashInfer autotune temporarily disabled for correctness (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41524">#41524</a>).</li>
<li><strong>DSV4</strong>: Improved fused Indexer Q quant kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41428">#41428</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>API &amp; Frontend</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Responses API</strong>: Streaming tool/function calling with <code>required</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40700">#40700</a>) and named tool/function choice (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41110">#41110</a>), resubmitting output items with missing fields (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41355">#41355</a>).</li>
<li><strong>OpenAI compatibility</strong>: <code>system_fingerprint</code> field in responses (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40537">#40537</a>), <code>prompt_embeds</code> content part support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40720">#40720</a>), <code>defer_loading</code> and <code>tool_reference</code> support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40190">#40190</a>), rendered prompt text in chat completion response (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42052">#42052</a>), tolerate empty content in forced tool choice (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40148">#40148</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Tool calling</strong>: XGrammar 0.2.0 with structural tags for strict tool calling + reasoning (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40894">#40894</a>), Cohere reasoning/tool parsers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40422">#40422</a>), LFM2/2.5 tool parser (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39243">#39243</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Tokenizer</strong>: Fastokens support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41741">#41741</a>).</li>
<li><strong>RLHF</strong>: Explicit <code>/start_weight_update</code> and <code>/finish_weight_update</code> APIs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39212">#39212</a>).</li>
<li><strong>ASR</strong>: Engine request abort on cancellation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41266">#41266</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Configuration</strong>: <code>VLLM_SKIP_MODEL_NAME_VALIDATION</code> env var (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34676">#34676</a>), configurable model weights loading tracking (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41086">#41086</a>), Triton JIT compilation monitor (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40137">#40137</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Build &amp; Dependencies</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Breaking</strong>: C++20 required for PyTorch compatibility (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40380">#40380</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Breaking</strong>: Transformers v4 deprecated (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40389">#40389</a>).</li>
<li>Docker image size reduced by ~2.5 GB via deferred FlashInfer cubin download (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41134">#41134</a>).</li>
<li>CUDA 13.0 wheels switched to PyTorch manylinux_2_28 base (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41416">#41416</a>).</li>
<li>DeepGEMM bundled wheel built per-Python for CPython compatibility (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41516">#41516</a>).</li>
<li>Container image provenance metadata embedded (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40653">#40653</a>).</li>
<li>tpu-inference upgraded to v0.19.0 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41844">#41844</a>).</li>
<li>NIXL connector bumped to 1.x (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42364">#42364</a>).</li>
<li>ROCm 7.2.2 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41386">#41386</a>).</li>
</ul>

### [v0.20.2](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.20.2)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>vLLM v0.20.2</h1>
<h2>Highlights</h2>
<p>This release features 6 commits from 6 contributors (0 new)!</p>
<p>This is a small patch release with bug fixes for DeepSeek V4, gpt-oss, and Qwen3-VL</p>
<h3>Bug Fixes</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>DeepSeek V4 sparse attention</strong>: Re-enable the persistent topk path on Hopper and ensure the memset kernel runs at CUDA graph capture time regardless of <code>max_seq_len</code>, fixing the MTP=1 hang on DeepSeek V4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41665">#41665</a>, revert of <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41605">#41605</a>).</li>
<li><strong>DeepSeek V4 KV cache</strong>: Fixed a "failure to allocate KV blocks" error in the V1 engine KV cache manager (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41282">#41282</a>).</li>
<li><strong>gpt-oss MXFP4 + torch.compile</strong>: Plumbed <code>hidden_dim_unpadded</code> through the <code>moe_forward</code> fake op so MXFP4 works under <code>torch.compile</code> on v0.20.x (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/42002">#42002</a>, backport of <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41646">#41646</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Qwen3-VL</strong>: Removed an invalid deepstack boundary check that could fail under heavy load (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40932">#40932</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h2>Contributors</h2>
<p><a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/ywang96">@ywang96</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/zyongye">@zyongye</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/stecasta">@stecasta</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/wzhao18">@wzhao18</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/Isotr0py">@Isotr0py</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/khluu">@khluu</a></p>

---

### [v0.20.1](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.20.1)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>vLLM v0.20.1</h1>
<p>This is a patch release on top of <code>v0.20.0</code> primarily focused on <strong>DeepSeek V4 stabilization and performance improvements</strong>, along with several important bug fixes.</p>
<h3>DeepSeek V4</h3>
<ul>
<li>Base model support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41006">#41006</a>).</li>
<li>Multi-stream pre-attention GEMM (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41061">#41061</a>), configurable pre-attn GEMM knob (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41443">#41443</a>), and tuned default <code>VLLM_MULTI_STREAM_GEMM_TOKEN_THRESHOLD</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41526">#41526</a>).</li>
<li>BF16 and MXFP8 all-to-all support for FlashInfer one-sided communication (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40960">#40960</a>).</li>
<li>PTX <code>cvt</code> instruction for faster FP32-&gt;FP4 conversion (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41015">#41015</a>).</li>
<li>Integrated tile kernels (<code>head_compute_mix_kernel</code>) for optimized head computation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41255">#41255</a>).</li>
<li>Guard megamoe flag with Pure TP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41522">#41522</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed persistent topk cooperative deadlock at TopK=1024 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41189">#41189</a>) and inter-CTA init race on RadixRowState (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41444">#41444</a>), with temporary disable of persistent topk as a workaround (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41442">#41442</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed import error due to AOT compile cache loading (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41090">#41090</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed torch inductor error (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41135">#41135</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed repeated RoPE cache initialization (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41148">#41148</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed missing type conversion for non-streaming tool calls in DSV3.2/V4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41198">#41198</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Bug Fixes</h3>
<ul>
<li>Fixed <code>max_num_batched_token</code> not being captured in CUDA graph (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40734">#40734</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed <code>num_gpu_blocks_override</code> not accounted for in <code>max_model_len</code> checks (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41069">#41069</a>).</li>
<li>Auto-disable <code>expandable_segments</code> around cumem memory pool (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40812">#40812</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed BailingMoE linear layer (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40859">#40859</a>) and MLA RoPE rotation for BailingMoE V2.5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41185">#41185</a>).</li>
<li>Fixed reasoning parser kwargs not being passed to structured output (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41199">#41199</a>).</li>
<li>[ROCm] Fixed <code>input_ids</code> and <code>expert_map</code> args for Quark W4A8 GPT-OSS (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41165">#41165</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h2>List of contributors</h2>
<p><a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/BugenZhao">@BugenZhao</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/chaunceyjiang">@chaunceyjiang</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/gau-nernst">@gau-nernst</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/ghphotoframe">@ghphotoframe</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/Isotr0py">@Isotr0py</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/jeejeelee">@jeejeelee</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/khluu">@khluu</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/njhill">@njhill</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/Rohan138">@Rohan138</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/wzhao18">@wzhao18</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/youkaichao">@youkaichao</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/ywang96">@ywang96</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/ZJY0516">@ZJY0516</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/zixi-qi">@zixi-qi</a>, <a class="user-mention notranslate" href="https://github.com/zyongye">@zyongye</a></p>
